Well… I guess it’s work, mostly. I’m an inspector, I’ve been doing quality checks for years, and I used to feel pretty steady about it. But we got this new operations manager, and he’s been picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve always used, like every little thing is suddenly wrong.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s not every single minute, no. I mean, I can do parts of the job and be fine for a while, especially the routine stuff… or at least I used to. But then if I know there’s going to be a meeting with him, or I’m looking at one of the procedures he’s been criticizing, it sort of comes up again.

Yeah… I mean, not exactly like I’m sitting on the couch thinking, “inspection procedures,” you know? But it follows me around. Ever since the new operations manager started picking apart the way I’ve done inspections for years, I just can’t seem to leave work at work.

Or… not even performance exactly, it’s like the way he questions the inspection steps I’ve used for years, and then I start wondering if I’m missing things I never used to worry about.\n\nI mean, I’ll come home and I’m still carrying it with me—wound up, short with my husband, replaying what was said in meetings. And then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls that used to be automatic. It’s not like I wake up anxious about everything in my life out of nowhere. It starts with work, and then it kind of spills over because I can’t shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety about work is constant during the workday, or does it come in waves, especially when you're thinking about specific interactions or tasks related to your new manager?\n\nPatient: It’s not every single minute, no. I mean, I can do parts of the job and be fine for a while, especially the routine stuff… or at least I used to. But then if I know there’s going to be a meeting with him, or I’m looking at one of the procedures he’s been criticizing, it sort of comes up again.\n\nAnd then I carry it home, which is the part that bothers me. I’ll be making dinner or talking with my husband and suddenly I’m back in my head replaying what was said, or I get snappy over something small. My husband put the mail in the wrong spot the other night and I acted like it was a huge thing, which it really wasn’t.\n\nSo I guess it comes in waves at work, tied to him and the inspection stuff, but once it gets going I have a hard time leaving it there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious about your work performance even when you're not actively thinking about specific interactions with your manager, like during routine tasks or outside of work hours?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but it still kind of starts from that whole situation with him. Like, I’ll be doing a routine quality call—something I’ve done for years without really thinking twice—and then suddenly I’m second-guessing myself, wondering if he’d say I missed a step or did it wrong.\n\nAnd then outside of work, it’s like I’m not sitting there calmly thinking, “Oh, my performance,” but I come home wound up. I’ll snap at my husband over nothing, and then later I’m lying awake replaying the meeting or what was said. So even when I’m not actively talking to him or thinking about one specific interaction, it’s still sort of… hanging over me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious about your work performance even when you're not doing anything related to the inspection procedures or thinking about your manager?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not exactly like I’m sitting on the couch thinking, “inspection procedures,” you know? But it follows me around. Ever since the new operations manager started picking apart the way I’ve done inspections for years, I just can’t seem to leave work at work.\n\nI’ll get home and I’m still wound up, and then I’ll snap at my husband over something stupid, like where something got put in the kitchen. And then I feel bad, and then somehow I’m back to replaying a meeting in my head. The next day, even routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble, I start second-guessing myself. So… yes, it’s not only when I’m actually doing the inspection steps or thinking directly about him. It kind of hangs over everything once it gets started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It’s mostly work that starts it, but it spills into home, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t say I’m suddenly worried about, I don’t know, the roof or the grocery bill all day… though I do get caught up in little things at home more than I used to.

And at work, it’s showing there too… I’ve missed some internal deadlines, and I’ve been pulling back from meetings I’m supposed to be in.

So, I guess it’s not separate from the job exactly, but it’s affecting the rest of my life.

So… yes, it’s not only when I’m actually doing the inspection steps or thinking directly about him. It kind of hangs over everything once it gets started.\n\nDoctor: Even though the anxiety seems to start with work, do you ever feel anxious about things unrelated to your job, like your personal life, relationships, or other responsibilities at home?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly work that starts it, but it spills into home, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t say I’m suddenly worried about, I don’t know, the roof or the grocery bill all day… though I do get caught up in little things at home more than I used to.\n\nLike our evenings used to have a rhythm—dinner, dishes, maybe sit down for a bit—and lately I’m not keeping up with that the same way. Then my husband and I end up arguing more, usually over things that probably shouldn’t turn into arguments. And at work, it’s showing there too… I’ve missed some internal deadlines, and I’ve been pulling back from meetings I’m supposed to be in.\n\nSo, I guess it’s not separate from the job exactly, but it’s affecting the rest of my life.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I think that’s the part that’s been frustrating, because I’ll try to do something normal, like sit down after dinner, and I’m not necessarily thinking, “work, work, work,” but I’m still wound up. Like my body hasn’t gotten the message that I’m home.

And then I’ll snap at my husband over some little thing, which isn’t really like me, and then I’m off on this whole loop. It reminds me of when my kids were younger and the house would get chaotic—except now there isn’t even anything happening, I’m just tense. And eventually it circles back to that manager picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve done for years, and I’m lying awake replaying meetings or thinking about how the next day I’ll probably second-guess even routine quality calls.

So… yes, I can be away from work and still not really relax. It all seems tied back to that situation, but it doesn’t stay neatly at work.

Then my husband and I end up arguing more, usually over things that probably shouldn’t turn into arguments. Like my body hasn’t gotten the message that I’m home.\n\nAnd then I’ll snap at my husband over some little thing, which isn’t really like me, and then I’m off on this whole loop. It reminds me of when my kids were younger and the house would get chaotic—except now there isn’t even anything happening, I’m just tense. And eventually it circles back to that manager picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve done for years, and I’m lying awake replaying meetings or thinking about how the next day I’ll probably second-guess even routine quality calls.\n\nSo… yes, I can be away from work and still not really relax. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… it keeps going even when I’m not actually at work. Like, I’ll be home and there’s no inspection procedure in front of me, nobody’s asking me anything, but I’m still wound up.

And then it turns into other things, sort of. I’ll snap at my husband over something small, and then I’m upset about that, and then my mind drifts right back to those meetings with the new operations manager picking apart how I’ve done inspections for years. It’s funny, not funny, but I used to be able to come home and just make dinner or watch something and be fine. Now I’ll lie awake replaying what was said, and the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I never used to think twice about.

So yes, I guess it doesn’t stay neatly attached to work, even though that’s where it started.

My husband put the mail in the wrong spot the other night and I acted like it was a huge thing, which it really wasn’t.\n\nSo I guess it comes in waves at work, tied to him and the inspection stuff, but once it gets going I have a hard time leaving it there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious about your work performance even when you're not actively thinking about specific interactions with your manager, like during routine tasks or outside of work hours?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but it still kind of starts from that whole situation with him. Like, I’ll be doing a routine quality call—something I’ve done for years without really thinking twice—and then suddenly I’m second-guessing myself, wondering if he’d say I missed a step or did it wrong.\n\nAnd then outside of work, it’s like I’m not sitting there calmly thinking, “Oh, my performance,” but I come home wound up. I’ll snap at my husband over nothing, and then later I’m lying awake replaying the meeting or what was said. So even when I’m not actively talking to him or thinking about one specific interaction, it’s still sort of… hanging over me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious about your work performance even when you're not doing anything related to the inspection procedures or thinking about your manager?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not exactly like I’m sitting on the couch thinking, “inspection procedures,” you know? But it follows me around. Ever since the new operations manager started picking apart the way I’ve done inspections for years, I just can’t seem to leave work at work.\n\nI’ll get home and I’m still wound up, and then I’ll snap at my husband over something stupid, like where something got put in the kitchen. And then I feel bad, and then somehow I’m back to replaying a meeting in my head. The next day, even routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble, I start second-guessing myself. So… yes, it’s not only when I’m actually doing the inspection steps or thinking directly about him. It kind of hangs over everything once it gets started.\n\nDoctor: Even though the anxiety seems to start with work, do you ever feel anxious about things unrelated to your job, like your personal life, relationships, or other responsibilities at home?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly work that starts it, but it spills into home, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t say I’m suddenly worried about, I don’t know, the roof or the grocery bill all day… though I do get caught up in little things at home more than I used to.\n\nLike our evenings used to have a rhythm—dinner, dishes, maybe sit down for a bit—and lately I’m not keeping up with that the same way. Then my husband and I end up arguing more, usually over things that probably shouldn’t turn into arguments. And at work, it’s showing there too… I’ve missed some internal deadlines, and I’ve been pulling back from meetings I’m supposed to be in.\n\nSo, I guess it’s not separate from the job exactly, but it’s affecting the rest of my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge even when work isn’t on your mind at all—like during leisure activities, while watching TV, or spending time with friends—where you just can’t seem to relax, even about things that used to feel routine or simple?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s the part that’s been frustrating, because I’ll try to do something normal, like sit down after dinner, and I’m not necessarily thinking, “work, work, work,” but I’m still wound up. Like my body hasn’t gotten the message that I’m home.\n\nAnd then I’ll snap at my husband over some little thing, which isn’t really like me, and then I’m off on this whole loop. It reminds me of when my kids were younger and the house would get chaotic—except now there isn’t even anything happening, I’m just tense. And eventually it circles back to that manager picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve done for years, and I’m lying awake replaying meetings or thinking about how the next day I’ll probably second-guess even routine quality calls.\n\nSo… yes, I can be away from work and still not really relax. It all seems tied back to that situation, but it doesn’t stay neatly at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety often continues even when the work-related stressor is not present, such as on days off or during activities that used to help you relax, and does it often shift to other concerns even if they’re not directly related to your job?\n\nPatient: Yes… it keeps going even when I’m not actually at work. Like, I’ll be home and there’s no inspection procedure in front of me, nobody’s asking me anything, but I’m still wound up.\n\nAnd then it turns into other things, sort of. I’ll snap at my husband over something small, and then I’m upset about that, and then my mind drifts right back to those meetings with the new operations manager picking apart how I’ve done inspections for years. It’s funny, not funny, but I used to be able to come home and just make dinner or watch something and be fine. Now I’ll lie awake replaying what was said, and the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I never used to think twice about.\n\nSo yes, I guess it doesn’t stay neatly attached to work, even though that’s where it started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s been… about two months now, I think. Basically since the new operations manager started changing my inspection procedures.

At first I thought it was just one rough week, you know, like when there’s a new person and everyone has to adjust. I remember telling myself that when my kids were in school and schedules changed, we’d all be cranky for a bit and then it settled down. But this hasn’t really settled.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.22, F43.23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Your Name]. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess it’s work, mostly. I’m an inspector, I’ve been doing quality checks for years, and I used to feel pretty steady about it. But we got this new operations manager, and he’s been picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve always used, like every little thing is suddenly wrong.\n\nAnd I know work changes, I do, I’ve seen plenty of managers come and go—one of them years ago had us redo the whole sorting area over a weekend, which was ridiculous—but this feels different. I can’t seem to leave it there anymore. I come home all wound up, I snap at my husband over nothing, and then I’m lying awake replaying meetings in my head. Then the next day I’m second-guessing calls I used to make without even thinking twice. So… yeah, that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about things outside of work, like everyday responsibilities, health, or other aspects of your life, even when there isn’t a clear problem?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say I sit around worrying about everything. Like bills, groceries, my health—well, I did have this one time I thought the washing machine was making a weird noise and I got all worked up about whether it was going to flood the laundry room, but that was probably because my sister had that happen years ago and it ruined her floor.\n\nBut generally… no, I don’t think it’s like that. It’s more that once something from work gets in my head, it sort of follows me around. Other things just get mixed in after that, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety is mostly about your work performance, or does it tend to spread to other areas of your life even when work isn’t on your mind?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly work performance, I think. Or… not even performance exactly, it’s like the way he questions the inspection steps I’ve used for years, and then I start wondering if I’m missing things I never used to worry about.\n\nI mean, I’ll come home and I’m still carrying it with me—wound up, short with my husband, replaying what was said in meetings. And then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls that used to be automatic. It’s not like I wake up anxious about everything in my life out of nowhere. It starts with work, and then it kind of spills over because I can’t shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety about work is constant during the workday, or does it come in waves, especially when you're thinking about specific interactions or tasks related to your new manager?\n\nPatient: It’s not every single minute, no. I mean, I can do parts of the job and be fine for a while, especially the routine stuff… or at least I used to. But then if I know there’s going to be a meeting with him, or I’m looking at one of the procedures he’s been criticizing, it sort of comes up again.\n\nAnd then I carry it home, which is the part that bothers me. I’ll be making dinner or talking with my husband and suddenly I’m back in my head replaying what was said, or I get snappy over something small. My husband put the mail in the wrong spot the other night and I acted like it was a huge thing, which it really wasn’t.\n\nSo I guess it comes in waves at work, tied to him and the inspection stuff, but once it gets going I have a hard time leaving it there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious about your work performance even when you're not actively thinking about specific interactions with your manager, like during routine tasks or outside of work hours?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but it still kind of starts from that whole situation with him. Like, I’ll be doing a routine quality call—something I’ve done for years without really thinking twice—and then suddenly I’m second-guessing myself, wondering if he’d say I missed a step or did it wrong.\n\nAnd then outside of work, it’s like I’m not sitting there calmly thinking, “Oh, my performance,” but I come home wound up. I’ll snap at my husband over nothing, and then later I’m lying awake replaying the meeting or what was said. So even when I’m not actively talking to him or thinking about one specific interaction, it’s still sort of… hanging over me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious about your work performance even when you're not doing anything related to the inspection procedures or thinking about your manager?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not exactly like I’m sitting on the couch thinking, “inspection procedures,” you know? But it follows me around. Ever since the new operations manager started picking apart the way I’ve done inspections for years, I just can’t seem to leave work at work.\n\nI’ll get home and I’m still wound up, and then I’ll snap at my husband over something stupid, like where something got put in the kitchen. And then I feel bad, and then somehow I’m back to replaying a meeting in my head. The next day, even routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble, I start second-guessing myself. So… yes, it’s not only when I’m actually doing the inspection steps or thinking directly about him. It kind of hangs over everything once it gets started.\n\nDoctor: Even though the anxiety seems to start with work, do you ever feel anxious about things unrelated to your job, like your personal life, relationships, or other responsibilities at home?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly work that starts it, but it spills into home, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t say I’m suddenly worried about, I don’t know, the roof or the grocery bill all day… though I do get caught up in little things at home more than I used to.\n\nLike our evenings used to have a rhythm—dinner, dishes, maybe sit down for a bit—and lately I’m not keeping up with that the same way. Then my husband and I end up arguing more, usually over things that probably shouldn’t turn into arguments. And at work, it’s showing there too… I’ve missed some internal deadlines, and I’ve been pulling back from meetings I’m supposed to be in.\n\nSo, I guess it’s not separate from the job exactly, but it’s affecting the rest of my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge even when work isn’t on your mind at all—like during leisure activities, while watching TV, or spending time with friends—where you just can’t seem to relax, even about things that used to feel routine or simple?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s the part that’s been frustrating, because I’ll try to do something normal, like sit down after dinner, and I’m not necessarily thinking, “work, work, work,” but I’m still wound up. Like my body hasn’t gotten the message that I’m home.\n\nAnd then I’ll snap at my husband over some little thing, which isn’t really like me, and then I’m off on this whole loop. It reminds me of when my kids were younger and the house would get chaotic—except now there isn’t even anything happening, I’m just tense. And eventually it circles back to that manager picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve done for years, and I’m lying awake replaying meetings or thinking about how the next day I’ll probably second-guess even routine quality calls.\n\nSo… yes, I can be away from work and still not really relax. It all seems tied back to that situation, but it doesn’t stay neatly at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety often continues even when the work-related stressor is not present, such as on days off or during activities that used to help you relax, and does it often shift to other concerns even if they’re not directly related to your job?\n\nPatient: Yes… it keeps going even when I’m not actually at work. Like, I’ll be home and there’s no inspection procedure in front of me, nobody’s asking me anything, but I’m still wound up.\n\nAnd then it turns into other things, sort of. I’ll snap at my husband over something small, and then I’m upset about that, and then my mind drifts right back to those meetings with the new operations manager picking apart how I’ve done inspections for years. It’s funny, not funny, but I used to be able to come home and just make dinner or watch something and be fine. Now I’ll lie awake replaying what was said, and the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I never used to think twice about.\n\nSo yes, I guess it doesn’t stay neatly attached to work, even though that’s where it started.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge even when there’s no clear reason or trigger for it, like when you’re alone by yourself or doing something that used to feel calming or routine?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ll be alone or just doing something ordinary, and I’m still on edge. It’s like I can’t leave work at work anymore.\n\nIt really started after this new operations manager came in and started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. And I know that sounds specific to work, but then I come home still wound up, and I’ll snap at my husband over nothing, and then I’m lying awake replaying the meetings. The next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without even thinking.\n\nSo even if there isn’t a clear trigger right in that moment, it’s like it’s still sitting there under everything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way? Has the anxiety been present most days and building up over time, or does it still feel tied to specific moments since the new manager started?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about two months now, I think. Basically since the new operations manager started changing my inspection procedures.\n\nAt first I thought it was just one rough week, you know, like when there’s a new person and everyone has to adjust. I remember telling myself that when my kids were in school and schedules changed, we’d all be cranky for a bit and then it settled down. But this hasn’t really settled.\n\nSo yes, it’s been most of these last two months, but it still feels connected to that starting point—him coming in and changing how I’m supposed to do the work I’ve done for years.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you feel anxious even when work isn’t on your mind at all—like when you’re alone, doing something you enjoy, or spending time with people you care about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. But this hasn’t really settled.\n\nSo yes, it’s been most of these last two months, but it still feels connected to that starting point—him coming in and changing how I’m supposed to do the work I’ve done for years.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you feel anxious even when work isn’t on your mind at all—like when you’re alone, doing something you enjoy, or spending time with people you care about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, even if I’m not actively thinking about work, it’s like it follows me home anyway.\n\nI’ll be trying to just have a normal evening, and then I’m wound up without meaning to be. It all started after this new operations manager came in and started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t know that I’d call it hopeless or anything like that. I think I’ve been so focused on trying to get through the day and not get pulled into my own head that I haven’t really stopped to sort out, “Is this sadness, is this just stress,” you know?

I mean, I still do my usual things, more or less. Sometimes I’m quieter, I guess, but then I start thinking about whether that’s just because I’m tired of talking about the same work mess over and over. My husband will ask what’s wrong and I’ll say “nothing,” and then I realize that’s not very helpful. But I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed losing interest in everything or feeling hopeless. It’s more like I’m just preoccupied.

I'm Dr. [Your Name]. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess it’s work, mostly. I’m an inspector, I’ve been doing quality checks for years, and I used to feel pretty steady about it. But we got this new operations manager, and he’s been picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve always used, like every little thing is suddenly wrong.\n\nAnd I know work changes, I do, I’ve seen plenty of managers come and go—one of them years ago had us redo the whole sorting area over a weekend, which was ridiculous—but this feels different. I can’t seem to leave it there anymore. I come home all wound up, I snap at my husband over nothing, and then I’m lying awake replaying meetings in my head. Then the next day I’m second-guessing calls I used to make without even thinking twice. So… yeah, that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about things outside of work, like everyday responsibilities, health, or other aspects of your life, even when there isn’t a clear problem?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say I sit around worrying about everything. Like bills, groceries, my health—well, I did have this one time I thought the washing machine was making a weird noise and I got all worked up about whether it was going to flood the laundry room, but that was probably because my sister had that happen years ago and it ruined her floor.\n\nBut generally… no, I don’t think it’s like that. It’s more that once something from work gets in my head, it sort of follows me around. Other things just get mixed in after that, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety is mostly about your work performance, or does it tend to spread to other areas of your life even when work isn’t on your mind?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly work performance, I think. Or… not even performance exactly, it’s like the way he questions the inspection steps I’ve used for years, and then I start wondering if I’m missing things I never used to worry about.\n\nI mean, I’ll come home and I’m still carrying it with me—wound up, short with my husband, replaying what was said in meetings. And then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls that used to be automatic. It’s not like I wake up anxious about everything in my life out of nowhere. It starts with work, and then it kind of spills over because I can’t shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety about work is constant during the workday, or does it come in waves, especially when you're thinking about specific interactions or tasks related to your new manager?\n\nPatient: It’s not every single minute, no. I mean, I can do parts of the job and be fine for a while, especially the routine stuff… or at least I used to. But then if I know there’s going to be a meeting with him, or I’m looking at one of the procedures he’s been criticizing, it sort of comes up again.\n\nAnd then I carry it home, which is the part that bothers me. I’ll be making dinner or talking with my husband and suddenly I’m back in my head replaying what was said, or I get snappy over something small. My husband put the mail in the wrong spot the other night and I acted like it was a huge thing, which it really wasn’t.\n\nSo I guess it comes in waves at work, tied to him and the inspection stuff, but once it gets going I have a hard time leaving it there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious about your work performance even when you're not actively thinking about specific interactions with your manager, like during routine tasks or outside of work hours?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but it still kind of starts from that whole situation with him. Like, I’ll be doing a routine quality call—something I’ve done for years without really thinking twice—and then suddenly I’m second-guessing myself, wondering if he’d say I missed a step or did it wrong.\n\nAnd then outside of work, it’s like I’m not sitting there calmly thinking, “Oh, my performance,” but I come home wound up. I’ll snap at my husband over nothing, and then later I’m lying awake replaying the meeting or what was said. So even when I’m not actively talking to him or thinking about one specific interaction, it’s still sort of… hanging over me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious about your work performance even when you're not doing anything related to the inspection procedures or thinking about your manager?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not exactly like I’m sitting on the couch thinking, “inspection procedures,” you know? But it follows me around. Ever since the new operations manager started picking apart the way I’ve done inspections for years, I just can’t seem to leave work at work.\n\nI’ll get home and I’m still wound up, and then I’ll snap at my husband over something stupid, like where something got put in the kitchen. And then I feel bad, and then somehow I’m back to replaying a meeting in my head. The next day, even routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble, I start second-guessing myself. So… yes, it’s not only when I’m actually doing the inspection steps or thinking directly about him. It kind of hangs over everything once it gets started.\n\nDoctor: Even though the anxiety seems to start with work, do you ever feel anxious about things unrelated to your job, like your personal life, relationships, or other responsibilities at home?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly work that starts it, but it spills into home, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t say I’m suddenly worried about, I don’t know, the roof or the grocery bill all day… though I do get caught up in little things at home more than I used to.\n\nLike our evenings used to have a rhythm—dinner, dishes, maybe sit down for a bit—and lately I’m not keeping up with that the same way. Then my husband and I end up arguing more, usually over things that probably shouldn’t turn into arguments. And at work, it’s showing there too… I’ve missed some internal deadlines, and I’ve been pulling back from meetings I’m supposed to be in.\n\nSo, I guess it’s not separate from the job exactly, but it’s affecting the rest of my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge even when work isn’t on your mind at all—like during leisure activities, while watching TV, or spending time with friends—where you just can’t seem to relax, even about things that used to feel routine or simple?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s the part that’s been frustrating, because I’ll try to do something normal, like sit down after dinner, and I’m not necessarily thinking, “work, work, work,” but I’m still wound up. Like my body hasn’t gotten the message that I’m home.\n\nAnd then I’ll snap at my husband over some little thing, which isn’t really like me, and then I’m off on this whole loop. It reminds me of when my kids were younger and the house would get chaotic—except now there isn’t even anything happening, I’m just tense. And eventually it circles back to that manager picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve done for years, and I’m lying awake replaying meetings or thinking about how the next day I’ll probably second-guess even routine quality calls.\n\nSo… yes, I can be away from work and still not really relax. It all seems tied back to that situation, but it doesn’t stay neatly at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety often continues even when the work-related stressor is not present, such as on days off or during activities that used to help you relax, and does it often shift to other concerns even if they’re not directly related to your job?\n\nPatient: Yes… it keeps going even when I’m not actually at work. Like, I’ll be home and there’s no inspection procedure in front of me, nobody’s asking me anything, but I’m still wound up.\n\nAnd then it turns into other things, sort of. I’ll snap at my husband over something small, and then I’m upset about that, and then my mind drifts right back to those meetings with the new operations manager picking apart how I’ve done inspections for years. It’s funny, not funny, but I used to be able to come home and just make dinner or watch something and be fine. Now I’ll lie awake replaying what was said, and the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I never used to think twice about.\n\nSo yes, I guess it doesn’t stay neatly attached to work, even though that’s where it started.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge even when there’s no clear reason or trigger for it, like when you’re alone by yourself or doing something that used to feel calming or routine?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ll be alone or just doing something ordinary, and I’m still on edge. Yeah… I think it’s more like I can’t leave work at work. I’ll tell myself I’m not thinking about him or those procedure changes, and then I’m home and I’m still wound up anyway.

I mean, I can do parts of the job and be fine for a while, especially the routine stuff… or at least I used to. Like, I’ll be doing a routine quality call—something I’ve done for years without really thinking twice—and then suddenly I’m second-guessing myself, wondering if he’d say I missed a step or did it wrong.\n\nAnd then outside of work, it’s like I’m not sitting there calmly thinking, “Oh, my performance,” but I come home wound up. I’ll snap at my husband over nothing, and then later I’m lying awake replaying the meeting or what was said. It kind of hangs over everything once it gets started.\n\nDoctor: Even though the anxiety seems to start with work, do you ever feel anxious about things unrelated to your job, like your personal life, relationships, or other responsibilities at home?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly work that starts it, but it spills into home, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t say I’m suddenly worried about, I don’t know, the roof or the grocery bill all day… though I do get caught up in little things at home more than I used to.\n\nLike our evenings used to have a rhythm—dinner, dishes, maybe sit down for a bit—and lately I’m not keeping up with that the same way. Then my husband and I end up arguing more, usually over things that probably shouldn’t turn into arguments. And at work, it’s showing there too… I’ve missed some internal deadlines, and I’ve been pulling back from meetings I’m supposed to be in.\n\nSo, I guess it’s not separate from the job exactly, but it’s affecting the rest of my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge even when work isn’t on your mind at all—like during leisure activities, while watching TV, or spending time with friends—where you just can’t seem to relax, even about things that used to feel routine or simple?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s the part that’s been frustrating, because I’ll try to do something normal, like sit down after dinner, and I’m not necessarily thinking, “work, work, work,” but I’m still wound up. Like my body hasn’t gotten the message that I’m home.\n\nAnd then I’ll snap at my husband over some little thing, which isn’t really like me, and then I’m off on this whole loop. It all seems tied back to that situation, but it doesn’t stay neatly at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety often continues even when the work-related stressor is not present, such as on days off or during activities that used to help you relax, and does it often shift to other concerns even if they’re not directly related to your job?\n\nPatient: Yes… it keeps going even when I’m not actually at work. Like, I’ll be home and there’s no inspection procedure in front of me, nobody’s asking me anything, but I’m still wound up.\n\nAnd then it turns into other things, sort of. I’ll snap at my husband over something small, and then I’m upset about that, and then my mind drifts right back to those meetings with the new operations manager picking apart how I’ve done inspections for years. It’s funny, not funny, but I used to be able to come home and just make dinner or watch something and be fine. Now I’ll lie awake replaying what was said, and the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I never used to think twice about.\n\nSo yes, I guess it doesn’t stay neatly attached to work, even though that’s where it started.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge even when there’s no clear reason or trigger for it, like when you’re alone by yourself or doing something that used to feel calming or routine?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ll be alone or just doing something ordinary, and I’m still on edge. Yeah, I guess I do. I mean, it’s hard for me to separate it out, because even when I think I’m not thinking about work, it’s like my body still is, if that makes sense.

I can’t seem to leave it there anymore. I come home all wound up, I snap at my husband over nothing, and then I’m lying awake replaying meetings in my head. Then the next day I’m second-guessing calls I used to make without even thinking twice. So… yeah, that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about things outside of work, like everyday responsibilities, health, or other aspects of your life, even when there isn’t a clear problem?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say I sit around worrying about everything. Like bills, groceries, my health—well, I did have this one time I thought the washing machine was making a weird noise and I got all worked up about whether it was going to flood the laundry room, but that was probably because my sister had that happen years ago and it ruined her floor.\n\nBut generally… no, I don’t think it’s like that. It’s more that once something from work gets in my head, it sort of follows me around. Other things just get mixed in after that, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety is mostly about your work performance, or does it tend to spread to other areas of your life even when work isn’t on your mind?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly work performance, I think. Or… not even performance exactly, it’s like the way he questions the inspection steps I’ve used for years, and then I start wondering if I’m missing things I never used to worry about.\n\nI mean, I’ll come home and I’m still carrying it with me—wound up, short with my husband, replaying what was said in meetings. And then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls that used to be automatic. It’s not like I wake up anxious about everything in my life out of nowhere. It starts with work, and then it kind of spills over because I can’t shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety about work is constant during the workday, or does it come in waves, especially when you're thinking about specific interactions or tasks related to your new manager?\n\nPatient: It’s not every single minute, no. I mean, I can do parts of the job and be fine for a while, especially the routine stuff… or at least I used to. But then if I know there’s going to be a meeting with him, or I’m looking at one of the procedures he’s been criticizing, it sort of comes up again.\n\nAnd then I carry it home, which is the part that bothers me. I’ll be making dinner or talking with my husband and suddenly I’m back in my head replaying what was said, or I get snappy over something small. My husband put the mail in the wrong spot the other night and I acted like it was a huge thing, which it really wasn’t.\n\nSo I guess it comes in waves at work, tied to him and the inspection stuff, but once it gets going I have a hard time leaving it there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious about your work performance even when you're not actively thinking about specific interactions with your manager, like during routine tasks or outside of work hours?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but it still kind of starts from that whole situation with him. Like, I’ll be doing a routine quality call—something I’ve done for years without really thinking twice—and then suddenly I’m second-guessing myself, wondering if he’d say I missed a step or did it wrong.\n\nAnd then outside of work, it’s like I’m not sitting there calmly thinking, “Oh, my performance,” but I come home wound up. I’ll snap at my husband over nothing, and then later I’m lying awake replaying the meeting or what was said. So even when I’m not actively talking to him or thinking about one specific interaction, it’s still sort of… hanging over me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious about your work performance even when you're not doing anything related to the inspection procedures or thinking about your manager?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not exactly like I’m sitting on the couch thinking, “inspection procedures,” you know? But it follows me around. Ever since the new operations manager started picking apart the way I’ve done inspections for years, I just can’t seem to leave work at work.\n\nI’ll get home and I’m still wound up, and then I’ll snap at my husband over something stupid, like where something got put in the kitchen. And then I feel bad, and then somehow I’m back to replaying a meeting in my head. The next day, even routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble, I start second-guessing myself. So… yes, it’s not only when I’m actually doing the inspection steps or thinking directly about him. It kind of hangs over everything once it gets started.\n\nDoctor: Even though the anxiety seems to start with work, do you ever feel anxious about things unrelated to your job, like your personal life, relationships, or other responsibilities at home?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly work that starts it, but it spills into home, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t say I’m suddenly worried about, I don’t know, the roof or the grocery bill all day… though I do get caught up in little things at home more than I used to.\n\nLike our evenings used to have a rhythm—dinner, dishes, maybe sit down for a bit—and lately I’m not keeping up with that the same way. Then my husband and I end up arguing more, usually over things that probably shouldn’t turn into arguments. And at work, it’s showing there too… I’ve missed some internal deadlines, and I’ve been pulling back from meetings I’m supposed to be in.\n\nSo, I guess it’s not separate from the job exactly, but it’s affecting the rest of my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge even when work isn’t on your mind at all—like during leisure activities, while watching TV, or spending time with friends—where you just can’t seem to relax, even about things that used to feel routine or simple?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s the part that’s been frustrating, because I’ll try to do something normal, like sit down after dinner, and I’m not necessarily thinking, “work, work, work,” but I’m still wound up. Like my body hasn’t gotten the message that I’m home.\n\nAnd then I’ll snap at my husband over some little thing, which isn’t really like me, and then I’m off on this whole loop. It reminds me of when my kids were younger and the house would get chaotic—except now there isn’t even anything happening, I’m just tense. And eventually it circles back to that manager picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve done for years, and I’m lying awake replaying meetings or thinking about how the next day I’ll probably second-guess even routine quality calls.\n\nSo… yes, I can be away from work and still not really relax. It all seems tied back to that situation, but it doesn’t stay neatly at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety often continues even when the work-related stressor is not present, such as on days off or during activities that used to help you relax, and does it often shift to other concerns even if they’re not directly related to your job?\n\nPatient: Yes… it keeps going even when I’m not actually at work. Like, I’ll be home and there’s no inspection procedure in front of me, nobody’s asking me anything, but I’m still wound up.\n\nAnd then it turns into other things, sort of. I’ll snap at my husband over something small, and then I’m upset about that, and then my mind drifts right back to those meetings with the new operations manager picking apart how I’ve done inspections for years. It’s funny, not funny, but I used to be able to come home and just make dinner or watch something and be fine. Now I’ll lie awake replaying what was said, and the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I never used to think twice about.\n\nSo yes, I guess it doesn’t stay neatly attached to work, even though that’s where it started.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge even when there’s no clear reason or trigger for it, like when you’re alone by yourself or doing something that used to feel calming or routine?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ll be alone or just doing something ordinary, and I’m still on edge. It’s like I can’t leave work at work anymore.\n\nIt really started after this new operations manager came in and started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. And I know that sounds specific to work, but then I come home still wound up, and I’ll snap at my husband over nothing, and then I’m lying awake replaying the meetings. The next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without even thinking.\n\nSo even if there isn’t a clear trigger right in that moment, it’s like it’s still sitting there under everything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way? Has the anxiety been present most days and building up over time, or does it still feel tied to specific moments since the new manager started?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about two months now, I think. Basically since the new operations manager started changing my inspection procedures.\n\nAt first I thought it was just one rough week, you know, like when there’s a new person and everyone has to adjust. I remember telling myself that when my kids were in school and schedules changed, we’d all be cranky for a bit and then it settled down. But this hasn’t really settled.\n\nSo yes, it’s been most of these last two months, but it still feels connected to that starting point—him coming in and changing how I’m supposed to do the work I’ve done for years.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you feel anxious even when work isn’t on your mind at all—like when you’re alone, doing something you enjoy, or spending time with people you care about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, even if I’m not actively thinking about work, it’s like it follows me home anyway.\n\nI’ll be trying to just have a normal evening, and then I’m wound up without meaning to be. My husband will say something small and I snap, and then I feel awful because that’s not really about him. It all started after this new operations manager came in and started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. And then I’m lying there later replaying those meetings, thinking about what I should’ve said, or whether I’m doing things wrong now.\n\nIt reminds me a little of when we’d have big changes at home and everyone would be tense for a while, except this is… it’s sticking around more. Even the next day, I’ll second-guess routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble. So yes, it’s not always front and center in my mind, but it’s still kind of running underneath everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your mood or emotions outside of the anxiety, such as feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t know that I’d call it hopeless or anything like that. I think I’ve been so focused on trying to get through the day and not get pulled into my own head that I haven’t really stopped to sort out, “Is this sadness, is this just stress,” you know?\n\nI mean, I still do my usual things, more or less. Sometimes I’m quieter, I guess, but then I start thinking about whether that’s just because I’m tired of talking about the same work mess over and over. My husband will ask what’s wrong and I’ll say “nothing,” and then I realize that’s not very helpful. But I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed losing interest in everything or feeling hopeless. It’s more like I’m just preoccupied.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge most days, even when you’re not thinking about work or your manager at all—like a constant background worry or tension that doesn’t go away?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s more like I can’t leave work at work. I’ll tell myself I’m not thinking about him or those procedure changes, and then I’m home and I’m still wound up anyway.\n\nIt’s funny, my husband will just ask some normal thing—like whether I picked something up at the store—and I’ll snap at him, and then I’m standing there thinking, “Why did I do that?” Because it’s not him. It’s all this stuff from the day still sitting in my chest.\n\nAnd then at night I’ll replay meetings in my head, what he said about the inspections, what I should’ve said back… and by the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without even thinking. So I don’t know if I’d call it constant exactly, but it’s there in the background a lot, even when I’m trying not to focus on work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or restless even during times when you're not thinking about work at all—like when you're alone, doing something you enjoy, or spending time with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. I mean, it’s hard for me to separate it out, because even when I think I’m not thinking about work, it’s like my body still is, if that makes sense.\n\nI’ll be home, just trying to have dinner or do something normal, and I’m still wound up. My husband says something completely ordinary and I snap, and then I’m upset with myself because he didn’t do anything. It goes back to this new operations manager… he’s been picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, and I keep replaying all of it.\n\nI’ll lie there at night going over meetings, what he said, what I should’ve said, and then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any problem. So, yes, even when I’m not sitting there thinking, “work, work, work,” it’s like it’s still following me around.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
